Index Definitions
Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

Bloomberg Commodity Index designed to provide liquid and diversified exposure to commodities via futures contracts. Is calculated on an excess return basis and reflects commodity
futures price movements.
MSCI Emerging Markets Index captures large and mid cap representation across 24 Emerging Markets countries. With 1,205 constituents, the index covers approximately 85% of the free
float adjusted market capitalization in each country.
MSCI Japan Index is designed to measure the performance of the large and mid cap segments of the Japanese market.

MSCI Pacific ex Japan Index captures large and mid cap representation across 4 of 5 Developed Markets (DM) countries in the Pacific region (excluding Japan). With 93 constituents, the index
covers approximately 85% of the free float-adjusted market capitalization in each country.
MSCI All-Country World Index is a premier benchmark representing global equity performance, covering large and mid-cap stocks across 23 developed and 24 emerging markets.

MSCI World Index ex U.S. captures large and mid cap representation across 22 of 23 Developed Markets countries--excluding the United States..
MSCI USA Momentum Index is based on MSCI USA Index, its parent index, which captures large and mid-cap stocks of the U.S. market. It is designed to reflect the performance of an equity
momentum strategy by emphasizing stocks with high price momentum, while maintaining reasonably high trading liquidity, investment capacity and moderate index turnover.

U.S. Large-cap Growth/Russell 1000 Growth Index measures the performance of the large-cap growth segment of the U.S. equity universe. It includes those Russell 1000 companies with
relatively higher price-to-book ratios, higher I/B/E/S forecast medium term (2 year) growth and higher sales per share historical growth (5 years).
U.S. Small-cap/Russell 2000 Index measures the performance of the small-cap segment of the U.S. Equity universe.
MSCI USA High Dividend Yield Index is designed to reflect the performance of equities in the MSCI USA Index (excluding REIT s) with higher dividend income and quality characteristics than

average dividend yields that are both sustainable and persistent.
U.S. Mid Cap/Russell Midcap Index measures the performance of the mid-cap segment of the U.S. Equity universe. The Russell Midcap Index is a subset of the Russell 1000® Index. It
includes approximately 800 of the smallest securities based on a combination of their market cap and current index membership.
MSCI USA Value Index captures large and mid-cap U.S. securities exhibiting overall value style characteristics.

MSCI USA Equal Weighted Index represents an alternative weighting scheme to its market cap weighted parent index, the MSCI USA Index. At each quarterly rebalance date, all index
constituents are weighted equally, effectively removing the influence of each constituent’s current price.
MSCI USA Minimum Volatility Index aims to reflect the performance characteristics of a minimum variance strategy applied to the large and mid-cap USA equity universe.

Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

ADVANCED ECONOMIES
EUROPEAN UNION
Stronger trade posture pays off. The EU and China have agreed to
restrain hybrid cars exports to the EU to a level half or less than cur -
rently projected over the next four years, cutting exports by “several
million units” though still allowing them to rise. China also agreed to

drop its tariffs on EUR 4 bn of EU exports and provide easier access to
Chinese rare earths and magnets under a fast-track licensing sche -
me. These announcements were made at the conclusion of EU Trade
Commissioner Maroš Šefcovic’s visit to Beijing, following a recent es -
calation in trade tensions. Meanwhile, France and Germany released
a joint non-paper on Strengthening European resilience, addressing
global macroeconomic imbalances and market distorting practices

calling notably for:
i) Broadening the tools the European Commission may use to address
unfair trade practices (including undervalued exchange rates) or im -
port surges;
ii) Procedural changes to make them easier to use (qualified majority
of member states needed to block rather than authorise them). These
measures will be discussed during the next EU council on October
15-16th.
The European Commission also unveiled a new critical materials pac -

kage involving 46 strategic extraction, processing or recycling projects
spread across 16 member states (including 5 in France). The goal is to
reduce the EU’s dependence on China for critical metals.
EUROZONE
Price pressures are broadening, activity holds up. Producer price in -
flation accelerated further in August (8.3% y/y 1 from 5.8%), driven by
energy. Pressures are becoming more widespread, notably in capital
goods and above all intermediate goods (6.8%). They are partly offset

by non-durable consumer goods, which remain in deflation (-0.8%).
Given the PPI's close correlation with the HICP , this points to further
upside risks to consumer inflation. On the activity front, final September
PMIs confirmed the flash estimates, with the composite index still solid
at 53.1 and the manufacturing index stable at 52.7. The construction
PMI improved but remains depressed (43.4; +0.4 pt). Retail sales edged
up 0.1% m/m2 in August.

France
Modest spread relief as budget positioning starts. The spread between
French and German 10Y yields narrowed to 136 bps 3 on October 9
(mostly thanks to a decline in the French yield). As the Finance Com -
mittee of the National Assembly started discussing the 2027 budget,
the government reiterated its strong intent not to deviate from the
5% of GDP deficit target and to get a budget adopted in time, likely
without a vote. This is the most likely outcome — but not to be im -

plemented before 10 December (given mandatory timelines) — which
effectively removes any incentive for MPs to back unpopular measures
during the process. We can therefore expect heated debates (including
multiple no-confidence votes being tabled but rejected) and a lack in
fiscal discipline. In parallel, the "Rassemblement National" party ("Na-
tional Rally" in English) presented its fiscal strategy, which includes an
alternative budget for 2027 and an aggressive fiscal consolidation path

throughout 2032. The party called for a referendum to constitutionalise
1 Year-on-year
2 Month-on-month
3 Basis points
a “golden rule” that would require a deficit at least 0.5 pp below the
debt-stabilising threshold (roughly 3% of GDP), as long as the public
debt ratio exceeds 60% of GDP . The golden rule would require a budget
balance adjustment of 0.5 pp per year if the fiscal deficit is more than
1 pp above the stabilising threshold. The adjustment measures put

forward lack specifics but likely overstate savings, understate costs
and rely on overly optimistic growth assumptions (despite large fiscal
consolidation, at least 1.7% annually over the next three years — a
pace reached only 4 times in France since 2008, apart from post-pan-
demic reopening, and well in excess of the roughly 1% growth potential
estimated by most international institutions). That said, it is significant
that this party has chosen to fly the flag for fiscal discipline loud and

clear. Other parties should do the same.
Activity is holding up. New vehicle registrations rose 11% y/y in Sep -
tember, confirming a marked acceleration over the past three months
(+9.4% y/y in Q3). In Q3, growth came entirely from electric vehicles
(+112% y/y), whose share of registrations reached an all-time high
(38% in Q3, 41% in September). Export growth continued in August,
both in services (+5.2% y/y YTD) and in goods (+5.6% y/y YTD) — though

driven by prices rather than volume in the latter case. Manufacturing
output posted only a moderate rebound in August (+0.3% m/m; fol -
lowing cumulative declines of 2.8% over the previous three months),
limited by renewed supply constraints in Q3 (which limited produc -
tion at 31.5% of companies; +5 pp compared to H1). Industrial produc-
tion fell (-0.3% m/m) as a result of a decline in electricity production
(-2.4% m/m) driven by the heatwave. Credit growth remains dynamic.

Bank and market financing to NFCs saw steady growth in August (+4%).
New housing loans rebounded slightly in August (EUR 12.2 bn) fol -
lowing a relatively weak July, whilst interest rates continued to rise.
Consumer credit remained buoyant.
Upcoming: Banque de France monthly Business Survey (Monday), Bu-
siness creations in September (Friday).
Germany
Bullish official growth forecast upgrade despite mixed data. The
German government raised its 2026 GDP growth forecast to 1.3%

— the fastest pace since 2017 — from a prior estimate of 0.5%, citing
stronger-than-expected exports and a surge in government spending.
The 2027 forecast was also raised to 1.1% from 0.9%. Industrial pro -
duction was stronger than expected in August (+2.3% y/y versus -1.7%
in July; the highest level since March 2025 and higher than antici -
pated), driven mainly by strong growth in construction output, prima -
rily in public works (+10.5%; to the highest level since November 2021).

By contrast, manufacturing output fell by -0.2% (after +3.3% in July)
due to a decline in transport equipment production (lowest level since
April 2022). Likewise, new factory orders hit their lowest level in one
year in August (-10.6% m/m). However, this decline is mainly due
to a base effect. Export growth remains solid: +4.4% YTD, despite a
-0.8% m/m decline in August. Exports to the Eurozone continue to rise
(+6.3% y/y YTD), while exports to the U.S. have returned to positive

territory (+1.1%) and those to China continue to decline (-12.9%). The
trade surplus reached EUR 19.5 bn in August (+4% y/y YTD, despite a
-9.8% m/m decline).

UNITED STATES
Mild slowdown in services and more rate hikes signaled. The non-ma-
nufacturing ISM survey fell 0.5 pp4 to 54.9 in September due to a sharp
slowdown in the business activity component (down 5.2 pp to 56.5 pp,
still a robust expansion). Employment rose and supply pressures in -
tensified for the first time since June, while the price gauge reached

its highest level (74, up 1.4 pp) since 2022. Meanwhile, the minutes
of the September FOMC meeting confirmed the Fed’s hawkish outlook
and noted that the AI-buildout carried growing weight in the reading of
current inflation data and outlook. Governor Waller and St. Louis Fed
President Musalem mentioned the need for additional rate hikes while
remaining flexible on timing. The New York Fed survey of consumer in-
flation expectations showed them rising to 3.9%. We continue to expect

two more hikes, the next one coming in December.
Upcoming: September CPI (Wednesday), September PPI and Retail
Sales (Thursday), September Industrial Production (Friday).
JAPAN
Solid wage growth fails to lift household spending. Total wage growth
eased to 3.8% y/y in August (down 0.5 pp) as bonus payments stalled
(0% y/y, after 5.3% y/y in July). This allowed continued real wage growth
at 1.5% y/y, extending an uninterrupted run of positive readings in 2026

as administrative measures kept inflation in check. However, this did
not prevent real household spending from contracting in August for the
ninth consecutive month (-3.1% y/y).
EMERGING MARKETS
The IIF reports that nonresident portfolio flows to EMs turned negative
at -USD 26.3 bn in September, after USD 11.4 bn in August, the first net
outflow since June, as a hawkish Fed lifted US real yields and the USD.
Equity outflows reached -USD 19.2 bn, led by heavy foreign selling of

Korean equities, while debt flows turned negative at -USD 7.0 bn as
hard currency demand faded and EM credit spreads widened late in
the month. Further hikes from DM central banks will create additional
headwinds, but EM should remain resilient as long as global growth
persists.
ASIA
China
Yuan policy defence. Coinciding with the Beijing visit of the EU trade
commissioner, the People's Bank of China issued an unusually lengthy
formal statement rejecting claims that the yuan is undervalued and

defending its exchange-rate management by reiterating that it has a
‘floating exchange rate regime’ with reference to a basket of currencies.
It further argued that China's trade surpluses are driven by rising in -
dustrial competitiveness rather than currency under-devaluation and
that exchange-rate adjustments will not resolve structural global im -
balances. Nonetheless, it allowed the yuan to hit a four-year high vs.
the CFETS basket — bringing its year-to-date gain to 5.7%. The PBOC

set the USD/CNY's reference rate at 6.7330, the strongest level since
2023.
India
Monetary tightening. The RBI raised its repo rate by 25bp to 5.5%, its
first hike since February 2023, and shifted to a “calibrated tightening”
stance amidst rising inflationary pressures. The RBI introduced new
measures to stabilise the rupee, including direct dollar supplies to
state-owned oil companies and tighter restrictions on FX derivatives.
4 Percentage points

EUROPE, MIDDLE EAST, AFRICA
Central Europe
Cautious monetary policy stance amid mild inflation uptick. Poland’s
and Romania’s Central banks kept policy rates unchanged at 3.75% and
6.5%, respectively. Inflation accelerated in September, with a mild up-
tick in the Czech Republic and Hungary (respectively 2.5% and 1.6% y/y)
and a more entrenched increase in Poland (4% y/y). Inflation is now
above the official target in the Czech Rep. and Poland, but fuel price

caps in both countries should contain inflation dynamics.
Gulf
PMIs for September point for a firm but uneven non-oil recovery. Both
Saudi and UAE PMIs stood at 55.3, driven mostly by export orders in
the UAE and by domestic demand in Saudi Arabia. On the contrary,
Qatar’s PMI fell again (to 46.7), while Kuwait’s PMI also declined but
remained in expansionary zone (at 52.4).
Türkiye
Manufacturing production contracted by 1.6% m/m in August, the lar-

gest monthly decrease since the beginning of the year. On a y/y ba -
sis, it fell by 2.3%. Both domestic demand and exports seem to have
contributed negatively to this poor outcome. On a positive note, in
September, CPI inflation (1.9% m/m, 27.9% y/y) slowed more than the
market expected, and exports rebounded.
LATIN AMERICA
Inflationary pressures slightly up across the region. In Brazil, headline
CPI inflation accelerated to 4.6% y/y from 4.2% y/y in August (above the

central bank's 4.5% target), partly reflecting the reversal of a tempora-
ry electricity price discount in August. In Mexico, inflation rose to 3.5%
in September from 3.3% in August, while in Colombia it reached 6.3%,
slightly up from 6.2% in August. Inflation remained high in Chile but
was stable at 4.1%. In Peru, it was also slightly up (4.2% vs. 4.1%); the
central bank kept its policy rate unchanged at 4.25% for the thirteenth
consecutive month but maintained an upward bias amid rising inflation

and energy prices.
COMMODITIES
Energy: Limited relief. Continuation of skirmishes in the Gulf of Hormuz
and escalation of the conflict between the Houthis and a Saudi-led
coalition left oil prices fluctuating around $100/b for Brent. However,
the complex security situation has been driving the cost of shipping to
record high levels and the price of physical barrel to early April levels.
Late on Friday, President Trump announced an agreement with Russian

President Putin to release to global markets 4.8 million tonnes of die -
sel (roughly 8 days of European consumption), provided conditions in
refineries allow for it, of which 300,000 tonnes immediately. The White
House also announced a suspension of sanctions on Russia’s exports
of diesel until April 2027. Energy experts expect a de minimis price
impact.
European gas prices (TTF) rose by 11.5% over the week to 82 €/MWh.
EU gas inventories reached 73.6% of full capacities on October 11 th

(the official target is 80% at end October).
Food inflation rose sharply in September. The FAO’s Food Price Index
grew by 5.8% y/y, up from just 3% in August. This was driven by cereal
(+17% y/y) and sugar prices (+15%).

## Document 289: Financial conditions in the Eurozone_ a tightening temporarily tempered by credit volumes (2026-10-07)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Financial conditions in the Eurozone_ a tightening temporarily tempered by credit volumes (2026-10-07).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-07
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

7 October 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
FINANCIAL CONDITIONS IN THE EUROZONE: A TIGHTENING TEMPORARILY TEMPERED BY CREDIT VOLUMES
Thomas Humblot
*LATEST OBSERVATION: 5 OCTOBER 2026  **ESTIMATE
Since June 2026, the ECB has been publishing 1 a new indicator that helps to assess the transmission of its monetary policy to the economy:
the Broad Intermediation Gauge 2 (ECB-BIG). This new tool has revealed that, thus far, strong credit growth has limited the restrictive impact

of tightening financial conditions caused by rising interest rates in the Eurozone. However, this offsetting effect is beginning to fade.
The ECB-BIG: a new indicator for a broader view of financial conditions
The ECB-BIG complements the range of indicators used by the European Central Bank (ECB) to assess the effective transmission of its monetary
policy. This new tool provides a comprehensive view of how restrictive (or expansive) financial conditions are in the Eurozone. It stands out

for its approach that focuses more specifically on intermediation conditions. Therefore, the ECB-BIG provides a more comprehensive view than
most other financial condition indicators of financial conditions. In fact, it covers variables relating to the volume of bank lending to households
and businesses (the ‘volume’ component). Traditionally, this type of indicator tends to focus more on market variables (primarily interest and
exchange rates), share prices and corporate spreads. In addition, the ‘price’ component of the ECB-BIG is extended to include the cost of bank

loans, which is another new feature.
The rise in interest rates was temporarily offset by increased lending
By aggregating the variables relating to banking and non-banking intermediation conditions, the ECB-BIG shows that rising interest rates
(negative price effect) were offset in particular by lending volumes, which have continued to rise in 2026 (positive volume effect). A more
‘traditional’ indicator of financial conditions, the Macro-Finance FCI (MF-FCI) — which is, incidentally, included in the ECB-BIG — reveals a

much more pronounced tightening over the same period (see the orange curve in the chart). However, this offsetting of the price effect by the
volume effect is now beginning to wane, a sign that monetary policy is being effectively transmitted to the economy. Financial intermediaries
are gradually passing on the rise in rates to their customers (see the dotted green lines, which represent the composite indicators of the cost
of new loans to households and businesses, combining interest rates for different maturities), while lending volumes are falling. As a result,

between July and August 2026, seasonally adjusted flows of loans to households and businesses almost halved.
1 P . Lane, 2026, The Outlook for the Euro Area Economy, Background slides for Reuters NEXT Europe, London, 16 June 2026
2 A. Allayioti et al., 2026, The ECB-BIG index: tracking credit conditions in the euro area, ECB Economic Bulletin, Issue 5/2026
SOURCE: ALLAYIOTI AND AL. (2026), ECB, BLETZINGER ET AL. (2026), BNP PARIBAS ESTIMATES

In the longer term, financial conditions are expected to tighten, including as measured by the ECB-BIG
Despite the very sharp widening of sovereign spreads between 28 September and 2 October, financial conditions as measured by the
MF-FCI did not tighten, with sovereign yields actually falling over this period (with the exception of the OAT and the BTP), for example.

Nevertheless, the high volatility of the bond market, particularly in the sovereign segment, is likely to continue to affect financial
conditions. Looking slightly further ahead, the ECB’s key interest rate rises — the one that occurred on 16 September and another that we
anticipate for December 2026 (both +25 bp) — are likely to result in a more pronounced tightening. With a time lag of a number of weeks,
financial conditions, as defined by the ECB-BIG, are also expected to tighten. The price effect is likely to lead to a negative volume effect

on this occasion.

## Document 290: First Impressions_ NZ Consumers Price Index Q3 2026 and change of OCR call (2026-10-22)

Source PDF: all report pdf/First Impressions_ NZ Consumers Price Index Q3 2026 and change of OCR call (2026-10-22).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-10-22
Pages: 12
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

#inflation #newzealand
First Impressions: NZ Consumers Price Index Q3 2026 and change
of OCR call
By Satish Ranchhod
Senior Economist, Westpac NZ
09:19 October 22 2026 Share
Consumer prices rose 0.7% in the September quarter and 3.7% y/y. Core inflation
pressures are elevated and market pricing is primed for a 25bp OCR hike next week, which
we now expect.
Consumers Price Index, September quarter 2026 – key stats

### Page 2

Headline inflation
Quarterly change: +0.7% (prev: +1.5%)
Westpac forecast: +0.7%
RBNZ: +0.8%
Market median: +0.8%, range +0.5% to +1.2%

Annual change: +3.7% (prev: +4.1%)
Westpac forecast: +3.7%, RBNZ: +3.9%, Market: +3.8%

Non-tradables
Quarterly change: +1.0% (prev: +0.6%)
Westpac forecast: +1.3%
Annual change: +3.3% (prev: +3.4%)

Tradables
Quarterly change: +0.2% (prev: +2.7%)
Westpac forecast: -0.2%
Annual change: +4.3% (prev: +4.9%)

Consumer prices rose 0.7% in the September quarter. That saw the annual inflation rate easing to
3.7%, down from 4.1% in the year to June. 
Today’s result was in line with our forecast, but lower than the RBNZ’s forecast for 3.9% annual
inflation. 
However, under the surface, the details were more concerning. Measure of core inflation have taken a
step higher, with several closely watched measures now at or slightly above the top of the RBNZ’s
target band. 
We suspect that today’s result will leave the market continuing to price a strong chance of another
OCR hike at next Wednesday’s policy review. In that light, we think the RBNZ will regard this as the
path of least resistance, and we now expect that they will deliver (previously we expected that they
would pause and assess, before hiking at the December MPS).

What contributed to inflation in the September quarter?
Underpinning September’s 0.7% rise in consumer prices were some big price movements in both
directions. 
The largest move in prices this quarter was actually a sharp fall in fuel costs. Fuel prices aren’t low by
any stretch of the imagination. However, average prices for both petrol and diesel over the September
quarter were lower than in the June quarter when prices rocketed higher in the wake of the Middle East

### Page 3

conflict. In terms of specifics, the average price for 91 unleaded was 6.5% lower in September than in
the June quarter, while diesel prices were close to 16% lower. Those moves took 0.4ppts off inflation
this quarter. 
While prices at the pump weren’t as high as they were in the June quarter, fuel costs are still painfully
high. And that’s been bleeding through into higher prices for other goods and services. Of note, the
September quarter saw large increases in passenger transport costs, with international airfares up 14%
and domestic airfares up nearly 8%. 
The September quarter also saw the annual increases in local council rates (up 6.2%) and charges for
services like water supply (up 7.5%). That continues the run of large increases seen in recent years.
Lastly, the past few months saw a 1.0% rise in food prices, largely as a result of the seasonal rise in
prices for fresh vegetables. 
On the downside, we’ve seen continued softness in housing rents, which are the largest component of
the CPI, accounting for 10% of the basket. With low population growth and ample supply, rents were
flat in the September quarter and have effectively been flat since the start of this year. In annual terms,
rental inflation is the lowest it’s been in more than two decades, with regions like Wellington seeing
outright falls.

Annual and core inflation 
The annual inflation rate dropped back to 3.7% in the September quarter, down from 4.1% in the year
to June and the highest level in two years.
Looking under the surface, prices in the domestically oriented non-tradables group rose 1.0% over the
quarter, less than expected. That saw annual non-tradables inflation nudging down to 3.3% (from 3.4%
in the year to June). 
Non-tradables inflation has continued to run at above-average rates over the past year. That’s despite
the softness in economic growth and the labour market. In large part, that’s due to the continued
sizeable increases in administered prices, such as electricity charges which rose 11% over the past
year. 
However, prices in other areas have also been firm. Non-tradables excluding housing and utilities costs
(which also omits local council rates) was 3.4% for the year. In seasonally adjusted terms, quarterly
non-tradables inflation has been tracking around 0.8% for most of the past year. It was noted that in
sectors like construction, higher materials costs are adding to prices. Feedback from business we've
heard indicates that those higher materials costs are likely related to high oil prices. 
On the imported front, lower fuel costs saw tradable prices rising by just 0.2% over the September
quarter (though above our forecast for a small fall). That saw annual tradables inflation dropping back
to 4.3%, down sharply from the 4.9% rise in the year to June. 
While fuel costs have been a key driver for tradables inflation, under the surface, other imported
inflation pressures are looking firm. Excluding food and fuel costs, tradables prices were up 2.7% over
the past year. That’s the highest level in three years. 
With big swings in fuel costs, and concern that this is spilling over into operating costs more generally,
a key focus for the RBNZ is what’s been happening to measures of core inflation (which smooth
through quarter-to-quarter swings in individual prices and instead track the underlying trend in

### Page 4

Related articles
inflation). 
On this front we did some see worrying signs. Several closely watched measures of core inflation have
picked up, and now sit at the top of (or slightly above) the RBNZ target band. In terms of specifics, we
saw: 
Reinforcing this picture of firm underlying inflation pressures, a growing number of goods have been
increasing in price. That could be an early indication that businesses, even those with a limited
exposure to fuel costs, are starting to factor in expectations of continued high inflation when setting
prices.

Outlook 
While today’s result was lower than the RBNZ assumed in their September MPS projections, the
firmness in core inflation will give the RBNZ pause for thought ahead of their policy review on 28
October. Furthermore, with a sharp rise in petrol prices in recent weeks, September’s easing in annual
inflation is likely to be just a temporary reprieve. We expect headline inflation to reaccelerate back over
4% before the end of this year, and it’s set to remain above the RBNZ’s current forecast for most of
2027. 
Market pricing and views have coalesced around an October hike which we now suspect the MPC will
see as the path of least resistance. Core inflation measures are now close to or above 3%, which will
add fuel to arguments from the MPC hawks. We will have more detail in our MPR Preview later today,
including on the implications for the shape of the tightening cycle moving forward.

## Document 305: GLOBAL MARKET CALL_ Downgrading 'Go Global' To Market Weight

Source PDF: all report pdf/GLOBAL MARKET CALL_ Downgrading 'Go Global' To Market Weight.pdf
Report source: researchmail轉寄
Date: 2026-10-18
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: GLOBAL MARKET CALL: Downgrading 'Go Global' To Market Weight
Date:    3 hours ago
GLOBAL MARKET CALL: Downgrading 'Go Global' To Market Weight

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
GLOBAL MARKET CALL:
Downgrading 'Go Global' To
Market Weight
By Ed Yardeni & Toby Hearst • 18 Oct 2026
View in browser

### Page 2

Photo by Robert Nyman / Unsplash
We are downgrading the “Go Global” portfolio strategy from overweight to
market weight and raising “Stay Home” (in the US) from underweight to
market weight.
Overseas earnings remain impressive, but the rallies in emerging market
economies’ stock markets have been led by just a handful of AI-related
stocks. Last week's rout in South Korea showed how vulnerable such
concentration leaves these stock markets. Additionally, European

economies look set to struggle as France's fiscal problems loom, and
China's economy depends almost entirely on exports. Plus, the Middle
East war could flare up again before or after the US midterm elections on
November 3, keeping oil prices, inflation, and interest rates elevated.
Here's more:
I. Global Bonds
The global bond rout took a breather last week. Yields fell in all eight
major bond markets we track, led by the US (chart). The US 10-year

Treasury yield fell 16bps to 5.08%, even as the CPI rose 3.7% y/y and the
PPI jumped 6.3% in September. That suggests bond investors see these
rates as attractive relative to their long-term inflation expectations.
The spread between French and German 10-year yields has stabilized at
around 137bps, near its recent high (chart). Spreads for Italy, Greece,

### Page 3

Spain, and Portugal have eased slightly from their late-September peaks.
Contagion from France has eased for now. But France's own fiscal risk
premium hasn't. The National Assembly began debating France's 2027
budget on October 13. Meanwhile, Paris is burning!
II. Global Stocks
Brazil leads the country ETF rankings mtd, up 12.3% in dollars, ahead of
next Sunday's presidential runoff (chart). South Korea is at the bottom,
down 17.7%. The US is up 0.1%, ahead of the All Country World ex-US

ETF, down 1.9%.

### Page 4

Europe's share of the All Country World MSCI's market cap has fallen to
13.0%, from as high as 30.8% in 2007 (chart). It looks set to keep losing
ground for a while. The US MSCI’s share has firmed to 64.8%, up from
62.4% in March. The Emerging Markets MSCI’s share has held at 12.1%
despite the Korean selloff.
The ratio of the US MSCI to the All Country World ex-US MSCI has turned
up in recent weeks (chart). It remains below its early-2025 high.

### Page 5

III. Earnings & Valuation
The All Country World ex US MSCI's forward earnings is up 41.4% y/y,
while forward revenues is up 11.0% (chart). The global economy is doing
well.
Overseas, earnings have far outpaced stock prices this year. The All
Country World ex-US MSCI is up 10.5% ytd, while its forward earnings is
up 35.9% (chart). Its forward P/E has dropped 18.9% ytd.

### Page 6

The US MSCI's forward P/E is 19.1 versus 12.2 for the All Country World
ex-US, a 57% premium (chart). US profit margins justify about half of it.
The US forward profit margin is 16.8%, about a third higher than the All
Country World ex-US's 12.7%.
IV. China Focus
China remains a source of deflation for the global economy through its
massive trade surplus. Exports jumped 36.3% y/y in September to a
record high (chart). The trade surplus was $133.5 billion. Semiconductor

exports more than doubled during the first nine months of the year.
Booming exports are the only reason China's economy isn't imploding.

### Page 7

The domestic economy remains weak, weighed down by a rapidly aging
population and a property debacle. The Shenzhen Real Estate Stock
Price Index is testing a floor that has held since 2012 (chart). We would
worry if it broke below that level.
The stock market hasn't benefited from the export boom either, so Beijing
appears to be propping it up again. Regulators met on Thursday to
discuss stabilization measures, according to Bloomberg. They reportedly

urged companies and state funds to buy shares and asked some
investors to curb selling. Mainland A-shares, where Beijing's state-backed
funds do most of their buying, held up into mid-2026 before turning down
(chart). Offshore Chinese stocks have been falling since late 2025.

### Page 8

clause.

Editorial 
Opportunities from the bond sell-off  
Strategy summary: The global bond sell-off is creating tactical 
and medium-term opportunities. In the US, September’s yield 
surge has improved the risk -reward in 10 -year government 
bonds, presenting an attractive entry point for investors with a 
6-12-month horizon. We believe bond and money markets are 
overly hawkish about the Fed outlook, given disinflation is likely 
to return as impact of oil prices and tariffs ease next year. 
In equities, we stick to quality, especially sectors riding the AI -
led earnings boom. The upcoming Q3 earnings season is likely 
to show AI -investments are powering sectors beyond the 
technology and communications services, such as financials, 
materials and power and electrification. We remain constructive 
on the semiconductor and memory segments. We also expect 
the US Dollar to peak in the next few weeks along with short -
to-medium-term US bond yields. This creates opportunities for 
AUD corporate bonds, Emerging Market USD bonds and gold. 
Attractive risk -reward in US 10 -year government bonds.  
The US 10-year bond yield rose more than 50bps in September 
(biggest monthly rise since 2022) and 115bps this year, amid 
expectations of economic resilience, higher Fed rates, fuel -
driven inflation, AI-related capital demand and fiscal concerns. 
Valuations appear close to fair value, improving the 6-12-month 
risk-reward. Bond arithmetic suggests losses of less than 1% if 
the yield rises to 6%, vs. more than 10% gains if it falls to 4.5%. 
Resilient US economic activity, but softer -than-expected 
inflation. September data showed robust US employment 
(jobless claims, ADP) and core capital-goods orders. However, 
consumer confidence remains depressed, real disposable 
income is flat and households are drawing on savings to sustain 
spending. Against this divided backdrop, core PCE inflation - 
the Fed’s preferred gauge - was softer than expected at 0.2% 
m/m and 3.0% y/y. A revised method to measure PCE inflation 
also indicate that annual core PCE inflation was 35bps lower 
than previously reported, narrowing the gap with CPI inflation. 
Markets appear overly hawkish on the Fed. Money markets 
price about 85bps of tightening over the next 12 months. A 
strong labour market (September’s non -farm payrolls data is 
due tonight) may justify one or two further hikes, but aggressive 
tightening after the sharp rise in bond yields – which has 
already tightened financial conditions - could threaten financial 
stability. US small- and mid-cap equities have already fallen 8–
9% from recent peaks over the past couple of months, 
materially underperforming large caps, as energy and 
borrowing-cost concerns intensify. W hile some Fed speakers 
remain hawkish, we  agree with New York Fed President 
Williams’ that there is no urgency to tighten. We see little 
chance of an October Fed rate hike and expect inflation data 
for Sep-Nov determining whether the Fed moves in December. 
Stay with quality equities and AI beneficiaries.  US large 
caps have absorbed the rise in bond yields relatively well, 
supported by expectations of robust earnings growth led by AI-
related investment. We continue to favour quality companies 
and sectors benefiting from this AI cycle. Semiconductors and 
memory remain preferred exposures: structural AI demand 
should extend the memory upcycle, while long -term supply 
agreements provide better revenue visibility. The opportunity is 
also broadening beyond technology and communication 
services into financials, materials, power and electrification. 
Opportunities in AUD corporate bonds and gold.  The USD 
rebound alongside higher US yields offers a tactical opportunity 
to add higher -yielding Emerging Market USD and non -USD 
bonds. We remain positive on AUD corporate bonds, which 
offer a yield premium over comparable US credit. Our 
constructive medium-term AUD/USD view could add returns for 
USD-based investors over the next 6 –12 months. We would 
also use gold’s recent pullback to add exposure, supported by 
sustained Emerging Market central bank demand. We continue 
to view USD 4,000/oz as important technical support for gold.  
—  Rajat Bhattacharya

Standard Chartered Bank

Top client questions 
  Do you expect more RBA rate hikes after its less hawkish 
guidance? What is your view on the AUD and AUD bonds? 
Our view: We expect the RBA to leave rates unchanged, but 
retain the option to hike again should inflation intensify. We 
maintain our medium-term constructive AUD view and maintain 
our opportunistically bullish AUD corporate bonds idea. 
Rationale: The RBA’s forward guidance remains data dependent, 
explicitly allowing further tightening if needed as inflation remains too 
high.  At 4.60%, Australia’s cash rate is currently 60–85bp above the 
Fed’s 3.75–4.00% range. Thus, even with our two additional 25bp s 
Fed hikes expectation, Australian rates would hold a modest positive 
rate differential. We expect the AUD to strengthen once the Fed 
signals that US rates are near their peak, which should allow resilient 
Australian fundamentals to reassert themselves. Key risks are 
persistently high US yields, weaker China growth or a sharper 
deterioration in global risk sentiment. 
We believe risk-reward has become more attractive for AUD bonds 
following the bear -flattening of the AUD yield curve. Markets are 
already pricing in one more RBA hike through end-2027, presenting 
an attractive opportunity to earn a yield even as increasingly 
restrictive financial conditions reinforc e disinflation over time. 
Meanwhile, the positive interest rate differential with the US supports 
the currency outlook, supporting the case for AUD bonds. 
—  Vincent Tan, Senior Investment Strategist 
— Cedric Lam, Senior Investment Strategist 
— Iris Yuen, Investment Strategist 
  What does the incoming US data tell us about the path for 
US 10-year bond yields from here? 
Our view: The risk -reward in US 10 -year yields has turned 
attractive following their move higher amid incoming US data. 
Rationale: Recent US data has indeed started to show softer-than-
expected inflation. However, market technicals are likely temporarily 
delaying a move lower in bond yields, with reports suggesting the  
rise in yields is creating forced selling amongst hedge funds and real 
estate investment trusts. Nevertheless, we do not expect this to be 
an extended selloff. We have initiated an opportunistic bullish idea 
on US 10-year government bonds this week and continue to believe 
this idea offers an attractive risk/reward on a 6-12-month horizon as 
yields become attractive enough to entice buyer flow to return. On a 
broader allocation basis, we retain our structural preference for 3-7-
year maturities in bonds. 
— Cedric Lam, Senior Investment Strategist

### Page 5

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 2 October 2026

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  How should investors position in the GBP ahead of the UK 
budget announcement next month? 
Our view: We maintain a medium-term constructive GBP view, 
but keep near-term exposure measured ahead of the 28 October 
Budget. We would view any pre -Budget weakness as an 
opportunity to gradually build GBP exposure. 
Rationale: The government’s decision to adjust the pension triple 
lock from 2030 is an early indication of greater willingness to tackle 
longer-term spending pressures, reinforcing Chancellor Healey’s 
commitment to fiscal discipline and meeting the fiscal rules. Markets 
are likely to scrutinise the Budget closely for evidence that this 
discipline extends to near -term tax and spending decisions. A 
credible Budget should help contain pressure on UK government 
bond yields and support GBP. Any fiscal slippage remains a key risk.  
Near term, we prefer measured GBP exposure as elevated US yields 
and repricing towards further Fed tightening could continue to 
support the USD. Over the medium term, we expect GBP to rebound 
as the Fed eventually signals the peak of its tightening cycle. 
—  Vincent Tan, Senior Investment Strategist 
—  Iris Yuen, Investment Strategist 
  How would different US mid-term election outcome 
scenarios affect the outlook for US equities? 
Our view: We believe election outcomes will have a limited 
impact over the next 12 months. We remain positive on US 
equities driven by fundamental earnings growth.  
Rationale: Over the past 50 years, the ruling US President’s party 
has retained control of both chambers of Congress on only two 
occasions (1978 and 2002). All other elections result ed in an 
opposition sweep or a divided government.  
Based on the latest prediction markets, it appears that an opposition 
sweep (both the House and Senate flips from Republican to 
Democrats) is more likely than a divided government ( in which the 
House flips to Democrats , while the Senate remains under 
Republican control). A scenario where Republicans retain a majority 
is least likely per prediction markets. 
While mid -term elections may introduce near -term volatility, we 
caution against overreaction. Historically, the average return for the 
S&P 500 following an opposition sweep is 17% over the next 12 
months, but the average return following a divided government is not 
significantly worse, at 15%. We prefer to base our investment views 
on fundamental earnings growth, where investor attention should 
focus on the upcoming Q3 earnings season. 
—  Jason Wong, Senior Equity Analyst

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 2 October 2026

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  What is the outlook for  the memory chip and broader 
semiconductors industry? 
Our view: We remain positive on semiconductors and memory 
chips. Structural AI demand should prolong the memory 
upcycle while long-term agreements improve revenue visibility. 
Rationale: Recent industry commentary points to tighter memory 
supply-demand conditions through 2028, reinforcing our view that AI 
demand is likely to extend the memory upcycle. Furthermore, adoption 
of long-term agreements improves revenue and earnings visibility. 
Despite debates around “Pacing the Frontier”, we have seen a flurry 
of new model releases, along with the introduction of new consumer 
AI agents capable of comparing prices online and making 
purchases. These are early days, but the path is open for a business-
to-agent (B2A) economy where businesses increasingly interact with 
AI assistants acting on customers’ behalf. As these applications are 
increasingly inference intensive, wider adoption should support 
demand for memory, with its share of global semiconductor 
revenues projected to rise from 30.7% in 2025 to 66.7% in 2027. 
Against this backdrop, we maintain our Opportunistic idea on Global 
Semiconductors. Given memory  chips’ cyclical nature, investors 
should avoid overconcentration in memory names and maintain a 
diversified exposure across Big Tech and semiconductors. 
—  Ryan Goh, Investment Strategist 
  Does a US bond yield stress test raise the risk to your 
constructive view on US equities? 
Our view: We have a target for the S&P 500 index of 8400 over 
the next 12 months. A scenario of a sustained rise in the US 10-
year government bond yield to 5.6% could lead to negative 
returns from the current S&P500 level (7,666). 
Rationale: Our base case 12-month target for the S&P 500 is 8,400, 
which assumes 2027 S&P 500 EPS of USD 420 and a 20x forward 
P/E multiple. A rise in bond yields would have an impact on equity 
valuations, as a higher discount rate reduces the present value of 
future earnings. W e stress -test our target under different 10 -year 
yield and earnings scenarios.  
A rise in bond yields would arguably be associated with a stronger 
growth environment and hence, even higher earnings. However, 
assuming unchanged EPS at our base case of USD 420, a 
(sustained) rise in the 10 -year bond yield to 5.6% would lead to 
negative returns from the current level  if not offset by a further 
improvement in earnings growth . However, in o ur base case  we 
expect the 10-year yield is more likely to fall from here. 
—  Fook Hien Yap, Senior Investment Strategist

## Document 350: Quantum dictionary, part 1_ Decoding the fundamentals From superposition to decoherence, we unpack the concepts and technologies shaping the future of quantum computing. 2 min read

Source PDF: all report pdf/Quantum dictionary, part 1_ Decoding the fundamentals From superposition to decoherence, we unpack the concepts and technologies shaping the future of quantum computing. 2 min read.pdf
Report source: Bank of America
Date: 2026-10-01
Pages: 9
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

13030420     1

Transformation 
Quantum dictionary, part 1: Decoding the fundamentals 
21 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• What is quantum computing? How does it work? What is a qubit? And when will quantum reach its full potential? Quantum 
computing challenges our intuition and our understanding of what computers can do. In this two-part dictionary series, we 
decode key terms behind what could be the next major technological revolution. 
• Qubits are the basic building blocks of quantum computers, harnessing properties such as superposition and entanglement to 
process information. However, qubits are highly sensitive to their surroundings where disturbances can lead to errors in 
quantum computation, making error correction a key challenge for building quantum computers. 
• There is no single approach to building a quantum computer. Researchers and companies are pursuing a range of qubit 
technologies, from neutral atoms and photons to spin, superconducting, topological and trapped-ion qubits, while quantum 
computing itself is likely to increasingly work alongside classical computing and AI.

First, let’s lay the groundwork… 
Quantum computing  
Quantum computing uses qubits and quantum properties such as superposition (defined later on) to perform complex 
calculations and solve certain problems faster than classical computers.1 From a practical perspective, quantum computing could 
be used to tackle problems related to optimization, machine learning, simulation and factorization across a range of sectors, 
including autos and logistics, financial services, energy and climate, health and life sciences and national security. 
Current quantum computers can perform ‘just’ thousands of quantum operations. As the technology advances from thousands to 
millions, billions and eventually trillions of operations, quantum computers could begin to have commercial applications.  
• Millions: Financial services and security could experience some of the first use cases, such as threat detection, fraud 
prevention, derivatives pricing, bond trading and arbitrage. 
• Billions: Quantum could help power AI through advanced data generation capabilities.  
• Trillions: Quantum chemistry applications could emerge, including drug discovery, battery simulation and materials 
discovery. 
These are the publicly known applications, according to Oxford Quantum Circuits (OQC). And as quantum computing capabilities 
improve, some of these use cases could emerge sooner than anticipated.  
Now, we’ll tackle the core concepts… 
Qubits 
Qubits (quantum bits) are a quantum computer’s basic unit of information. They can exhibit quantum properties such as 
superposition and entanglement (which we elaborate on below) — properties fundamental to quantum computing. 
Quantum gates manipulate the state of qubits, much like logic gates manipulate bits in classical computers. Despite the name, a 
quantum gate is not a physical object, but rather an operation applied to a quantum system that changes its state. 
From physical to logical qubits 
Physical qubits are the foundation of a quantum computer’s hardware. However, they are highly susceptible to error. To address 
this, multiple physical qubits can be structured together to encode a logical qubit, allowing errors to be detected and corrected

1 Schneider, J., & Smalley, I. (2025, June 10). What is quantum computing? IBM. 
I NSTI TUTE
Accessible version

### Page 2

2 21 October 2026 
  I NSTI TUTE

and making the stored quantum information more reliable. In recent years, according to BofA Global Research, logical qubits 
have become a greater focus for quantum computing companies. 
Entanglement 
Entanglement is a condition where two or more qubits become linked so that their states are correlated, regardless of distance 
(Exhibit 1). It allows quantum computers to process information in ways that can be difficult or impossible for classical 
computers. 
So, how does it work? Suppose we have two photons that have opposite polarizations (the orientation of their light waves). 
Before measurement, neither has a definite polarization. When one is measured as horizontally polarized, the other will be 
vertically polarized when measured in the same basis, wherever it is. When measured separately, they appear to be random, but 
when compared, they are correlated (Exhibit 2). 
The challenges 
Once created, entanglement must be protected from decoherence (where interactions with the environment destroy quantum 
correlations). Maintaining entanglement over long distances is another challenge. Photons traveling through optical fiber can be 
absorbed or scattered, making long-distance quantum communication difficult. 2 Quantum repeaters aim to overcome this 
challenge by extending entanglement across a series of shorter links. 
Exhibit 1: Entanglement is a state where at least two qubits become 
correlated 
An infographic of entangled qubits

Source: BofA Global Research 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Exhibit 2: Measuring one qubit in an entangled pair instantly 
determines the state of the other, no matter the distance 
An example of what entanglement means for qubit measurement

Source: BofA Global Research 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Superposition 
Unlike a classical bit, which can be one of two states (0 or 1), superposition allows a quantum system to exist in multiple states 
or configurations at the same time (Exhibit 3). For example, a qubit can exist in the 0 state, the 1 state or any state that is a 
combination of 0 and 1.3  
A quantum system can maintain a superposition until it is observed or measured. Once measured, its superposition collapses into 
a single state. Another way of thinking about superposition is to imagine a sphere with the north pole representing zero and the 
south pole representing one. Other points on the surface of the sphere represent different superposition states. This allows us 
to: 1) represent complex forms of information; and 2) simulate atoms in a computer. 
Schrödinger’s cat 
In 1935, physicist Erwin Schrödinger proposed a thought experiment to illustrate the strange implications of quantum 
mechanics. In this scenario, a cat, a radioactive substance that decays very slowly, and a Geiger counter are put together in a 
sealed box. According to quantum mechanics, the system can be described as a superposition in which the cat is both alive and 
dead until the box is opened. Once the box is opened and the system is observed, the superposition collapses into a single 
outcome.

2 Rehman, M. (2026, May 11). What is Quantum Entanglement? Quantum Insider. 
3 Microsoft. (n.d.). Superposition.

### Page 3

21 October 2026    3

I NSTI TUTE

Enabling parallel computation versus classical computing 
Superposition is a key attribute of quantum computing that allows for quantum parallelism, or the ability to perform multiple 
computations at the same time. Classical bits can only be in one of two states, 0 or 1. In contrast, a qubit can be in a 
superposition of 0 and 1, allowing a quantum computer to process multiple possible qubit states at once.4  
Exhibit 3: Superposition is the condition where a qubit can exist in a superposition of 0 and 1 
Illustrating the power of superposition

Source: BofA Global Research 
Note: A Bloch sphere is a geometric representation of qubit states. 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Error rates and fidelity 
Fidelity is a measure of how closely a quantum state or operation matches what was intended (Exhibit 4). A higher fidelity 
generally means fewer errors. 
Physical qubits are prone to errors. One way to address this is to combine multiple physical qubits to create a more reliable 
logical qubit. Quantum error correction (QEC) allows errors in the physical qubits to be detected and corrected, helping protect 
the information stored in the logical qubit.

4 Ibid.

### Page 4

4 21 October 2026 
  I NSTI TUTE

Exhibit 4: Fidelity measures how accurately a qubit retains its intended quantum state during computation 
Infographic showing the implications of high versus low fidelity

Source: BofA Global Research  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
How does quantum error correction work? 
At its simplest, QEC relies on redundancy, or storing information across multiple qubits so that errors can be detected and 
corrected.  
Consider an example: suppose someone wants to send a message represented by 0. Instead of sending a single 0, the message 
could be repeated three times as 000, known as a codeword. If one bit were accidentally flipped from 0 to 1 during transmission, 
the recipient might receive 010. Because two of the three bits are still 0, the recipient could infer that the intended message 
was 000. 
However, if two bits flipped, producing 110 or 011, the recipient could infer the wrong message. If all three flipped to 111, the 
recipient would have no way to know from the received codeword that errors had occurred.5 This approach is known as a 
repetition code and illustrates how redundancy can help protect information from errors.  
Repetition codes are a simple form of error correction that can address bit-flip errors, where a 0 changes to a 1 or vice versa. 
More sophisticated QEC codes are needed to address other quantum errors, such as phase flips.6 Shor’s code, developed by 
American theoretical computer scientist Peter Shor, uses nine physical qubits to encode one logical qubit and can correct one 
bit-flip error or one phase flip error, but it can’t correct both types of errors at the same time. Meanwhile, Steane’s seven-qubit 
code, developed by Oxford physicist Andrew Steane, can also correct both types of errors. It has the advantage of being fault-
tolerant, meaning the error-correction process itself does not introduce additional errors.7 
At what error level might we start seeing quantum advantage? 
Some classical computing systems today operate with extremely high reliability, often described as having "17 nines" of fidelity 
(99.99999999999999999%). By comparison, quantum computers currently achieve fidelity rates of around 99.9%. Gerald 
Mullally, CEO of OQC, has argued that quantum systems would need to add roughly three more nines of fidelity to achieve 
commercial relevance, or quantum advantage. 
Noise and decoherence 
Noise refers to unwanted disturbances that affect quantum systems and can lead to errors in computations (Exhibit 5). It can 
come from sources such as interactions with the environment, electromagnetic interference and temperature fluctuations. 
Coherence is a quantum system’s ability to maintain its quantum properties, including superposition and entanglement. When 
coherence is lost through interactions with the environment, the process is known as “decoherence,” causing the system to 
behave more classically (Exhibit 6).8

5 Microsoft Ignite. (n.d.) Introduction to quantum error correction. 
6 Ibid. 
7 Ibid. 
8 Quandela. (n.d.) Quantum Decoherence.

### Page 5

21 October 2026    5

I NSTI TUTE

To fully control qubits for calculations and information transfer, they need to be carefully protected from their surroundings. 
External factors like noise, energy, changes in Earth’s magnetic field, radiation from Wi-Fi or mobile phones or cosmic rays can 
interfere with the process. Even small disturbances can lead to decoherence, making it difficult to develop large-scale, fault-
tolerant quantum computers.9 
According to QuEra Computing, quantum noise can be mitigated through physical isolation of qubits, the development of more 
precise control techniques and quantum error correction, which helps detect and correct errors in physical qubits.  
Exhibit 5: Noise refers to unwanted disturbances, e.g., interactions 
with the environment, that lead to errors in quantum computation 
An infographic explaining quantum noise

Source: BofA Global Research 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Exhibit 6: Decoherence is when qubits lose their key quantum 
properties, superposition and entanglement 
An infographic of decoherence

Source: BofA Global Research 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Quantum processing unit (QPU) 
Referred to as the brain of the quantum computer, the QPU is the core hardware where qubits reside and where quantum 
computations take place. QPUs rely on quantum behaviors like superposition and entanglement to tackle problems in areas such 
as cryptography, simulation and optimization. Just as a CPU (central processing unit) in classical computing contains multiple 
components, a QPU includes quantum gates (operations that manipulate qubits in a controlled way, similar to logic gates in 
classical computing) and interconnects, which enable communication between qubits and other quantum processors.10 
Next, we’ll look at broader computing architecture… 
Heterogeneous computing  
Heterogeneous computing refers to a future computing landscape likely combining classical and quantum computing, potentially 
unlocking more powerful, faster and cheaper compute and helping solve problems that classical computers cannot. According to 
BofA Global Research, AI and quantum computing could also converge and complement each other, enabling more 
transformative use cases. 
Quantum modality  
A quantum modality refers to the type of physical technology used to create and manipulate qubits. We discuss the modalities in 
greater depth in the following section. 
Lastly, we outline qubit technologies… 
Neutral atom qubit 
A quantum modality that uses individual atoms with no electrical charge as qubits (Exhibit 7). The atoms are held in place using 
focused lasers known as optical tweezers. Neutral atom qubit systems do not require atoms to be ionized, which means fewer 
energy constraints and potentially simpler cooling requirements. Atoms such as rubidium or cesium are typically used. The qubits 
are manipulated by changing the energy levels or positions of the trapped atoms using laser pulses.

9 QuEra. (n.d.) Quantum Noise. 
10 SpinQ. (2025, January 16). What does a Quantum Computer Look Like? Explain Key Parts.

### Page 6

6 21 October 2026 
  I NSTI TUTE

Exhibit 7: A quantum modality where atoms with a net electrical charge of zero are the foundation for qubits 
Key features of neutral atoms

Source: BofA Global Research 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Photonic qubit 
A quantum modality that uses particles of light, known as photons, to carry quantum information (Exhibit 8).11 Optical 
components such as beam splitters, which divide light along different paths, and phase shifters, which change the phase of light, 
can be used to perform quantum operations. Photonic quantum computers can use integrated photonic chips to manipulate 
photons and enable quantum logic. 
Exhibit 8: A quantum modality relying on the quantum properties of light 
Key features of photonic qubits

Source: BofA Global Research 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

11 Moore, J. (2025, March 28). The 6 different types of quantum computing technology. TechTarget.

### Page 7

21 October 2026    7

I NSTI TUTE

Spin qubit 
A quantum modality that uses the intrinsic spin of electrons or nuclei in solid-state materials, such as silicon or diamond. These 
spins can be manipulated using magnetic or electric fields, and their states are read using optical or charge-sensing techniques. 
Spin qubits are particularly appealing because they can leverage existing CMOS (complementary metal-oxide semiconductor; a 
technology to build processors) manufacturing infrastructure, making it easier to integrate them with conventional processors. 
Superconducting qubit 
A quantum modality in which electronic circuits create qubits. They are built using Josephson junctions, where two 
superconductors are separated by a thin insulating layer. Microwave pulses manipulate the qubits’ quantum states to run 
computations.12  
Specifically, a resistance-free current oscillates back and forth around a quantum loop. An injected microwave signal excites the 
current into superposition states.13 To enable this process, superconducting materials are cooled to near absolute zero (around 
10-20 millikelvin) using dilution refrigerators, helping reduce thermal noise and maintain coherence. 
Topological qubit 
A quantum modality that aims to encode quantum information using topological properties rather than relying only on individual 
particle states (Exhibit 9). One way to think about this is through the paths certain quasiparticles take as they move around one 
another, creating “braids” in space-time. Because the information can depend on the overall pattern of these braids rather than 
small details of their paths, the approach is designed to be more resilient to certain disturbances. 
Exhibit 9: Resilient to small perturbations but requires special quasiparticles not yet been conclusively demonstrated in nature 
An infographic of topological qubits

Source: BofA Global Research 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Trapped-ion qubit  
A quantum modality in which ions (electrically charged atoms) are held in place using electromagnetic fields. These ions act as 
the qubits and are controlled by the system with tightly focused laser beams.14 The qubits are held inside ultra-high vacuum 
(UHV) chambers.

12 Ibid. 
13 Simon Benjamin 
14 Moore, J. (2025, March 28). The 6 different types of quantum computing technology. TechTarget.

## Document 375: Threats to European diesel imports_ risk of shortages ruled out for the time being, but increased risk of price rises (2026-10-01)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Threats to European diesel imports_ risk of shortages ruled out for the time being, but increased risk of price rises (2026-10-01).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-01
Pages: 2

### Page 1

Paris, 1st October 2026

EcoFlash

Threats to European diesel imports: risk of shortages ruled out for the time being, but increased 
risk of price rises

In Europe, the possibility of a US ban on diesel exports has heightened concerns about price trends for this widely used 
fuel1, which has already risen significantly since the outbreak of the conflict in Iran. It has become a major political issue 
on both sides of the Atlantic in the run-up to decisive elections.

A particularly tight diesel market due to a shortage of buffer stocks 
Following the outbreak of the conflict, diesel prices did indeed see some of the sharpest rises on the oil market due to particularly 
tight market conditions. Since the end of February, whilst the price of a barrel of crude has risen by 46%, diesel prices have soared 
by 100% in Europe and by 76% in the United States.

The factors alleviating pressure on the refined products market, and more specifically the diesel market, were more limited than in 
the case of crude oil. Shipments passing through the Strait of Hormuz (around 17% of maritime diesel trade prior to the conf lict) 
have fallen by around 75%. Added to this is the halt in Russian exports due to war damage, which is depriving the diesel market of 
around 12% of its maritime exports. Furthermore, unlike with crude oil, China has not acted as a stabilising  force in the diesel 
market. On the contrary, the energy crisis has led to a halt in Chinese exports of refined products (including diesel) to the  rest of 
Asia from April onwards. The resumption of Chinese exports over the last two months remains fragile, given, in particular, that 
diesel stocks are at their lowest level in 15 months.

The United States has eased these tensions in the diesel market by increasing its exports of refined products (up 25% on average 
in the first half of 2026 compared with the average over the last five years). This rise in exports has required high refinery utilisation 
rate (close to maximum capacity) and, to a lesser extent, a reduction in stocks (-11% compared with the average over the last five 
years as at mid -September for middle distillates 2). All these factors have contributed to tightening the US domestic market and 
pushed retail diesel prices to record levels. Road diesel prices (US average) have risen by 68% since the end of February and by 
74% year-on-year.

A structurally high level of vulnerability in Europe… 
Against this market backdrop, characterised by mounting tensions and very limited room for manoeuvre , Europe would be 
particularly hard hit by a reduction in – or even a complete halt to – US exports. According to Eurostat, imports accounted for 46% 
of European demand for diesel in the first half of 2026, with 40% of these imports coming from the United States. Europe, which

1 Diesel accounts for 17% of the primary energy mix in the EU and 7.3% in the United States. 
2 A category of refined petroleum products comprising mainly diesel and aviation fuel.

### Page 2

has lost a great deal of production capacity in recent years, is operating its refineries at near -maximum capacity. Its room for 
manoeuvre is therefore limited.

… But the risk of a shortage has been ruled out in the short term 
The risk of a shortage, however, appears limited in the short term. The situation regarding European stocks is not comfortable, but 
it is not alarming. Commercial diesel stocks in the ARA region 3 are currently at historically low levels (around 12 million barrels), 
23% below their level at the end of September 2025. As for emergency stocks (EU Emergency Stocks), these are, according to the 
European Commission, at a sufficient level. Eurostat estim ates this figure at 270 million barrels in June 2026 (stable from a year 
earlier), representing approximately 60 days’ worth of EU consumption. Europe imports around 0.9 mb/d of diesel from the United 
States, and emergency stocks would therefore cover around 60 days’ consumption at 4.5 mb/d.

The rise in prices could be significant 
Whatever the outcome of the conflict, diesel prices are likely to continue to rise in the short term. According to our base -case 
scenario, crude oil prices are expected to remain very high in Q4 2026 due to ongoing tensions in the Persian Gulf, whilst a return 
to normal levels of diesel exports from Russia and the Middle East appears unlikely by that time.

Among the factors likely to cause future tensions, the start of the maintenance season in Europe could affect production leve ls, 
whilst demand for diesel in the United States is typically high in the autumn (agricultural harvests and pre -winter storage). I n a 
scenario where tensions remain low, the diesel refining margin (crack spread) could rise by 5% in Q4 compared with its level at the 
end of September 2026, before gradually falling back during 2027. An intermediate scenario (a 10% rise in the crack spread in Q4 
2026) would result in moderate constraints on US diesel exports and a drawdown of European stocks. Finally, an adverse scenario 
(a 35% rise in the crack spread in Q4 2026) would imply severe restrictions on US diesel exports and a delayed European response.

What impact will this have on inflation in the euro area? 
According to our estimates, the rise in diesel prices would exacerbate the expected peak in inflation in the euro area without altering 
its timing. In our baseline scenario for September 2026 (see our recently published EcoPerspective), headline inflation would peak 
at 4.1% y/y in January 2027 before gradually falling back towards 2% by the end of the year. The two least adverse scenarios 
would add only a limited amount to the peak: +0.1 pp in the first scenario (+5% for the crack spread in Q4 compared with the end 
of Q3) and +0.2 pp in the intermediate scenario (+10%), with inflation reaching 4.2% and 4.3% respectively in January 2027. 
However, there is an upside risk in the second scenario linked to the uncertain extent of the spillov er effects. In both cases, the 
main impact would be felt through fuel prices (which quickly reflect rising market prices) and would fade over the course of 2027. 
In the case of the adverse scenario (+35%), the additional inflation would be close to 0.6 pp by early 2027, pushing inflation above 
4.6%. A shock of this magnitude would, in fact, reduce businesses’ ability to absorb rising costs, which would lead to price increases 
across a broader range of goods (food, industrial goods). Subsequently, the effect would only fade gradually.

Pascal Devaux 
Economist, Middle East, Western Balkans – Energy 
pascal.devaux@bnpparibas.com

Guillaume Derrien 
Economist, Europe, Eurozone, International Trade 
guillaume.a.derrien@bnpparibas.com

3 Amsterdam-Rotterdam-Antwerp, Europe’s main hub for the storage of raw materials.

產業更新 – 半導體產業 
400G/lane 驅動下一代光調變與封裝升級 
What’s New - TFLN 邁入量產並開啟 400G/lane 封裝新週期 
TFLN 由技術驗證跨入商業量產階段，HyperLight、聯電(2303 TT)及聯穎光電共同擴大 TFLN 量
產平台，其中聯穎 6 吋產線已完成客戶驗證，聯電則導入 8 吋。產品端亦由 200G/lane 快速往
400G/lane 升級。相較矽基調變器在高速化後面臨頻寬、插入損耗及驅動電壓間的取捨，TFLN
利用 Pockels Effect具備高頻寬、低光損及低驅動電壓優勢，並有望降低獨立射頻Driver 需求。
隨 TFLN 頻寬提升至 100~150GHz 以上，封裝介面逐漸成為新瓶頸，開始導入 Flip-Chip 及凸
塊接合以縮短 Driver-to-Modulator 電性路徑，Gold Bump 亦有望受惠異質整合需求增加。 
Trends/Outlook - 高速光互連進入材料分工並帶動調變器升級 
展望 27~30 年，我們認為 TFLN 不會全面取代矽光子，而將優先切入對頻寬、功耗及訊號品質
要求較高的高速光調變器，形成 Si/SiN 負責被動光路、TFLN 負責主動調變、III-V 負責雷射及
CMOS 負責訊號處理的異質整合架構。26~27 年 1.6T/200G per lane 仍以矽光子為主流，
27~29 年進入 3.2T/400G per lane 後，TFLN 憑藉 130~150GHz 以上頻寬、低 Vπ 及低插入
損耗優勢有望加速滲透。隨產業由 6 吋往 8 吋 TFLN 量產發展，競爭焦點將逐步轉向成本、良
率及高速封裝能力 ， 並帶動Flip-Chip、RDL、Cu Pillar 及 Gold Bump 等晶圓級互連需求提升 。

Investment Summary 
1. TFLN 產業正處於由高性能元件走向可量產平台的關鍵轉折期，投資主軸可分為前段晶圓製
造與後段高速互連。聯電(2303 TT) 已正式加入 HyperLight 與聯穎的 TFLN 量產體系，負責將
既有 6 吋平台進一步擴展至 8 吋，同時公司本身亦具備 12 吋矽光子量產與 400G/lane 純矽平
台布局，因此並非單壓 TFLN 取代 Silicon，而是同時掌握 Si 與 TFLN 兩條技術路線，可在
200G/lane 維持矽光子量產優勢，並於 400G/lane 以上參與 TFLN 及 Si/TFLN 異質整合。 
2. 頎邦(6147 TT)則位於 TFLN 後段高速互連環節 ， 公司目前已透過矽光子切入PD、TIA、Driver
及 Modulator，並具備 Gold Bump 、Cu Pillar、RDL、晶圓測試及切割等完整後段能力。隨
TFLN 由 6 吋往 8 吋量產擴張 ， 以及400G/lane 以上產品提高 Driver-to-Modulator Flip-Chip
滲透率，頎邦既有晶圓級 Bumping 與互連平台有望直接受惠。

2 
TFLN 邁入量產階段並開啟 400G/lane 高速封裝新週期 
TFLN(Thin-Film Lithium Niobate， 薄膜鈮酸鋰)產業於 25~26年由技術驗證跨入商業量產階段 ，
產業發展重心亦由過去追求單一元件的超高頻寬，逐步轉向 8 吋晶圓製造、客戶認證、模組整合
及高速封裝。26 年 HyperLight、聯電(2303 TT)及聯穎光電共同擴大 TFLN 量產平台，其中聯穎
既有 6 吋產線已完成客戶驗證，聯電則進一步導入8 吋製造，代表TFLN 開始進入成熟晶圓代工
體系 ， 補足過去在晶圓均勻性 、 製程穩定度 、 良率及量產規模上的主要瓶頸 。 產品端亦快速升級 ，
HyperLight 目前 TFLN 發射光子晶片電光頻寬已達 130~150GHz 以上 ， 部分低壓版本差動驅動
電壓可低至約 0.6Vpp；26 年 OFC 更已展示 210GBaud、420Gbps/lane PAM4 的 TFLN 發射
光學次模組，產品藍圖則進一步指向448Gbps/lane，顯示 TFLN 已由 1.6T/200G per lane 逐步
進入 3.2T/400G per lane 世代。 
值得注意的是 ，400G/lane 並非矽光子的技術終點 ， 目前純矽MZM 亦已完成 400Gbps/lane 展
示，聯電也同步與 SILITH 開發 400G/lane 純矽光子平台。因此 TFLN 真正的優勢並非矽無法做
到 400G，而是隨傳輸速率提高，矽基調變器使用自由載子電漿色散效應，在頻寬、插入損耗、
驅動電壓及調變效率之間的取捨逐漸放大；相較之下，TFLN 利用 Pockels Effect，可同時維持較
高頻寬 、 較低光損及較低驅動電壓 ， 使其優勢逐漸由元件性能延伸至整體光模組功耗 。HyperLight
於 26 年展示的 1.6T DR8 參考模組整體功耗約 20W，低 Vπ特性使 TFLN 具備降低甚至取消獨
立射頻 Driver 的潛力 ， 加上較低插入損耗可減少雷射功率需求 ， 使TFLN 開始由高速光調變材料
轉變為改善整體功耗與物料成本的系統級方案。

圖一. Overall design of the broadband thin-film lithium niobate (TFLN)

資料來源: Photonics 2025, 12(5), 452、國泰證期研究部整理

另一方面，隨 TFLN 本體電光頻寬提升至 100~150GHz 以上 ， 封裝介面逐漸成為新的性能瓶頸 。
傳統 Driver 與 Modulator 透過印刷電路板及 Wire Bond 傳輸高速訊號，在 100GHz 以上容易
受到寄生電感、寄生電容及阻抗不連續影響，因此產業開始導入 Flip-Chip 與凸塊接合，縮短
Driver-to-Modulator 的高頻電性路徑。26 年已有多通道 TFLN Flip-Chip 平台完成 13 條高速
通道與 32 條低速通道整合，整體接合良率達 94%，顯示 TFLN 的商業化重點正由前段元件性能
進一步延伸至 2.5D/3D 封裝與晶圓級互連 。 不過TFLN 高速化並不等同於 Gold Bump 需求等比
例增加，實際接合材料仍可能採用 Gold Bump、Cu Pillar、Solder 等不同方案；因此真正受惠

### Page 3

投資 顧問 
產業報告

2026/10/21

3 
的是高速 Bumping 及晶圓級互連市場，而金凸塊則憑藉抗氧化、高延展性、低溫熱壓接合及異
質材料應力吸收能力，在 TFLN、Si 與 InP 等不同材料整合中具備潛在優勢。

高速光互連進入材料分工時代並帶動 TFLN 調變器升級  
展望 27~30 年，我們認為 TFLN 最可能的發展路徑並非全面取代矽光子，而是優先取代矽光子
平台中對速度 、 功耗及訊號品質最敏感的高速光調變器功能 ， 並逐步形成Si/SiN 負責被動光路 、
TFLN 負責高速主動調變、III-V 負責雷射、CMOS 負責電子訊號處理的異質整合架構。矽光子仍
具備成熟 12 吋晶圓製造、生態系、設計套件、低成本被動光波導及 Ge 光偵測器整合等優勢，
因此在 200G/lane 及部分 400G/lane 市場仍將保持主流 ； 但當產品進一步進入400G/lane 甚至
更高速世代，約 200GBaud 級訊號對頻寬、 Driver、光損及功耗要求快速提高， TFLN 的
130~150GHz 以上頻寬、低 Vπ 及低插入損耗將逐漸轉化為更具體的系統成本優勢。我們因此
認為 400G/lane 不是矽光子的終點，但可能是 TFLN 由高性能利基技術進入資料中心大量市場
的重要轉折點。

表 1. TFLN vs Silicon MZM 比較 
資料來源：國泰證期研究部整理 
從時間軸來看，26~27 年的 1.6T/200G per lane 預期仍以矽光子為主流，TFLN 主要透過低功
耗、降低 Driver 需求及改善光損切入部分高階產品；27~29 年進入 3.2T/400G per lane後，隨
高頻訊號餘裕與整體功耗預算進一步收窄，TFLN 的性能優勢有望加速轉化為商業價值。更長期
而言，我們預期市場將朝 TFLN-on-Si、TFLN-on-SiN 及其他異質整合平台發展，而不是單一材
料完全取代另一材料。目前 200mm TFLN/SiN 異質整合平台及 TFLN 整合至矽光子後段製程的
研究均已出現，顯示 TFLN 更可能以 Chiplet 或主動式光調變層的形式加入既有矽光子生態系。

投資 顧問 
產業報告

2026/10/21

4 
TFLN 量產規模亦將成為下一階段競爭重點 。 過去產業瓶頸主要在LNOI 晶圓供應 、 薄膜均勻性 、
蝕刻品質與良率 ， 目前HyperLight/聯穎/聯電 、Lightium 及其他供應商均逐步往 8 吋平台發展 ，
顯示 200mm TFLN 已成產業共同方向。當 8 吋晶圓供應與量產良率成熟後，競爭焦點將由是否
能生產轉向成本能否下降以及封裝是否能支援 100GHz 以上高頻介面 。 這將同步提高Flip-Chip、
RDL、Cu Pillar 及 Gold Bump 等晶圓級互連的重要性，尤其當 Driver-to-Modulator 電性路徑
必須由數百微米至毫米級 Wire Bond 縮短至數十微米時，封裝將從過去的後段支援製程，轉變
為決定 TFLN 能否實現 400G/lane 以上性能的核心環節。 
400G/lane 推動 TFLN 商業化 ， 聯電與頎邦分掌前後段關鍵環節 
我們認為 TFLN 產業目前正處於由高性能元件進入可量產平台的關鍵轉折期，投資主軸可分為前
段晶圓製造與後段高速互連兩個方向。聯電(2303 TT)已正式加入 HyperLight 與聯穎的 TFLN 量
產體系，負責將平台由 6 吋進一步擴展至 8 吋，同時公司本身亦具備 12 吋矽光子量產與
400G/lane 純矽平台布局。因此聯電並非只壓 TFLN 取代 Silicon，而是同時掌握 Si 與 TFLN 兩
條技術路線，可在 200G/lane 以矽光子取得量產規模、400G/lane 提供 Pure-Si 與 TFLN 雙平
台，並在更高速世代參與Si/TFLN 異質整合。我們認為其投資價值主要來自特殊製程光子晶圓代
工平台的擴張，而非單一 TFLN 產品貢獻。 
據供應鏈了解，目前美系客戶對 TFLN 的導入興趣明顯升溫，TFLN 需求正逐步擴散至更多光通
訊業者。隨 1.6T 產品量產及 3.2T/400G per lane 規格持續推進，客戶對低功耗、高頻寬及低驅
動電壓光調變器的需求提高，使 TFLN 開始成為美系高速光通訊廠商的重要技術評估方向。我們
認為，若後續更多美系客戶由送樣與驗證進一步轉入量產 將加速 8 吋 TFLN 晶圓及相關後段
Bumping 需求放量。 
頎邦(6147 TT)的受惠邏輯則位於 TFLN 後段製程，公司目前已透過矽光子切入 PD、TIA、Driver
及 Modulator，並具備 Gold Bump、Cu/Ni/Au、Cu Pillar、RDL、晶圓測試、研磨及切割等完
整後段能力，同時可支援6 吋與 8 吋晶圓製程。我們預期聯電TFLN 相關後段製程將主要交由頎
邦承接 ， 並預計自26 年年底開始貢獻營收 ， 因此無論既有6 吋 TFLN 需求或後續 8 吋平台放量 ，
頎邦皆有望直接受惠晶圓級 Bumping、RDL 及相關後段製程需求成長。隨400G/lane 以上產品
逐步提高 Driver-to-Modulator Flip-Chip 滲透率，頎邦既有高速 Bumping 與晶圓級互連能力
的重要性亦將進一步提升。 
整體而言，我們對 TFLN 產業的核心判斷是 400G/lane 不代表 Silicon 退出，而是 TFLN 的高頻
寬、低驅動電壓與低光損開始具備足以抵銷較高材料與製造成本的系統級價值。 若未來 DSP 直
接驅動、8 吋 TFLN 量產及 Driver-to-Modulator Flip-Chip 三項技術同步成熟，TFLN 有望優先
取得高速光調變器市場 ， 而Si/SiN 仍負責被動光路與大規模整合 。 聯電為目前較明確的前段TFLN
量產受惠者，頎邦則提供後續高速晶圓級互連的選擇權；後續最重要的觀察指標包括 8 吋 TFLN
良率與成本、400G/lane 產品實際量產時間、Driverless 架構滲透率，以及 TFLN 封裝由 Wire 
Bond 轉向 Flip-Chip 的速度。這些因素將分別決定 TFLN 產業的可服務市場，以及聯電與頎邦
最終能取得的實際營收與獲利貢獻。

### Page 3

中国
(株)日本総合研究所 中国経済展望 2026年9月2
景気は内需主因で停滞
◆消費・投資はコロナ禍以来の低い伸び
中国景気は内需の不振で足踏み 。小売売上高
は、財消費の低迷を主因に前年並みの水準で推移。
耐久消費財の買い替え補助金の効果がはく落して
いるほか、中東危機によるガソリン価格の高騰などを
背景に消費者マインドが一段と悪化 。固定資産投
資はインフラ投資などで底打ちの兆しがみられるもの
の、昨年以降大幅な減少が継続。
一方、外需は高い伸びを維持 。世界的なAIブー
ムを背景にIT関連財の輸出が引き続き好調である
ほか、EV需要の高まりを受けて自動車輸出台数も
3カ月連続で過去最高水準。もっとも、原油や半導
体などの価格上昇の影響も大きく 、実質ベースでは、
内需の減速を補うには至らず。
◆成長率目標の達成は困難
先行きの景気も、内需を中心に停滞が続く見通し。
ハイテク製造業の投資拡大や電力 ・通信網などの
「６つのネットワーク 」推進によるインフラ投資の持ち
直しが見込まれるものの 、不動産不況や過剰生産

能力といった構造的な下押し要因が残存し 、投資
は総じて低調に推移する見込み。悲観的な消費者
マインドが続くなか、消費も低迷が続く公算大。
一方、政府は景気刺激策に慎重 。成長率よりも
経済の質を重視し 、不動産市場や地方政府債務
などのリスク解消に注力する姿勢を明確にしており 、
2026年の成長率目標は達成困難な状況。
経済政策スタンス（鐘才文コラム）
（資料）人民網などを基に日本総研作成
（注）中央財経委員会が「鐘才文」名義で掲載した文章。
現状認識
・AI、ロボット、ハイテク製造業など新たな成長ドラ
イバーが台頭
・成長モデルの転換期にあり、成長率や月次指標
よりも経済運営の質を重視
・供給は強い一方、需要が弱い状況は残存
経済運営の
方向性
・AIや先端製造業を軸とした産業高度化を推進
・大規模な景気刺激策には依存しない
・不動産市場や地方政府債務などのリスク解消策
に注力（短期の成長率にはマイナスとなるも、長
期かつ持続的な経済発展にはプラス）
下半期の
経済見通し
・第15次５カ年計画などで確定した重点プロジェ

クトを執行すれば、経済は安定的に推移
